People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I worry about the usual things now and then, but I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your worries are more intense or more frequent than you’d like, or do you have trouble controlling your worry on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I can fret a little like anyone, but I wouldn’t say it gets away from me regular.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel down or lose interest in things you used to enjoy, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I have quiet days, but I wouldn’t say I feel down or stopped enjoying things. Nothing I’ve especially noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself moving around, fidgeting, or feeling like you can't sit still, even when you're not trying to get anything done?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. Like if I’m sitting through announcements at church, or at the senior center, I get sort of ants-in-my-chair. I’ll shift around, or get up for coffee, straighten papers… just something so I’m not sitting there so long.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you have to move around or do something, just to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. I’m always puttering around with something — fixing this, arranging that. Even when there’s no real need. Sitting still just doesn’t feel very natural to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness or need to move around ever cause you distress, or make it hard for you to do things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. At the senior center, I’ve had to apologize after meetings… I guess I come across too keyed up. One friend even said maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table because I was making things tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble focusing or keeping your attention on what you’re doing, even if it’s something simple like reading a pamphlet or listening to a conversation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really noticed that as much. I can usually follow along, I think. Sometimes my mind wanders a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like if I’m sitting through announcements at church, or at the senior center, I get sort of ants-in-my-chair. I’ll shift around, or get up for coffee, straighten papers… just something so I’m not sitting there so long.\n\nDoctor: Even when there’s nothing that needs to be done, do you still feel like you have to move around or do something, just to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. I’m always puttering around with something — fixing this, arranging that. Even when there’s no real need. Sitting still just doesn’t feel very natural to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness or need to move around ever cause you distress, or make it hard for you to do things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. At the senior center, I’ve had to apologize after meetings… I guess I come across too keyed up. One friend even said maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table because I was making things tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble focusing or keeping your attention on what you’re doing, even if it’s something simple like reading a pamphlet or listening to a conversation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really noticed that as much. I can usually follow along, I think. Sometimes my mind wanders a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself jumping into activities or making decisions quickly without thinking them through, like signing up for something new without considering how it fits into your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I’ll say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve really thought about whether I have the room for it. I just feel like I ought to be doing something, you know. It’s hard for me to just sit and leave things be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on tasks or conversations, even when they’re simple or important, like following directions or finishing something you start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I can lose my place now and then, but… I wouldn’t say focusing is one of the main problems for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on a task or conversation?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, if there’s a loud noise or something I’ll notice it, but I don’t think I’m easily pulled away most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

One friend even said maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table because I was making things tense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble focusing or keeping your attention on what you’re doing, even if it’s something simple like reading a pamphlet or listening to a conversation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I haven’t really noticed that as much. I can usually follow along, I think. Sometimes my mind wanders a little, but I wouldn’t say it’s a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself jumping into activities or making decisions quickly without thinking them through, like signing up for something new without considering how it fits into your schedule?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes. I’ll say yes to another volunteer thing before I’ve really thought about whether I have the room for it. I just feel like I ought to be doing something, you know. It’s hard for me to just sit and leave things be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on tasks or conversations, even when they’re simple or important, like following directions or finishing something you start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I can lose my place now and then, but… I wouldn’t say focusing is one of the main problems for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get easily distracted by things around you, even when you're trying to focus on a task or conversation?\n\nPatient: Not especially, no. I mean, if there’s a loud noise or something I’ll notice it, but I don’t think I’m easily pulled away most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you need less sleep than most people, or that you feel rested even if you don't sleep very long?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t say that. I usually seem to need my sleep like anyone else, I think. Nothing unusual that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ve had good moods and bad moods, of course, but nothing I’d call unusual for days or that other people really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt so good or so energetic that others noticed a big change in your mood or behavior, even for just a few days?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I can’t recall anyone saying I seemed that different for a few days or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when your thoughts seemed to race so much that it was hard to keep up, or when ideas were coming so fast that it was hard to organize them?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, my mind can be busy sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s racing so fast I can’t keep up.
